        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/experiments.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Experiments}\\label{sec:experiments}\n\nWe analyze how attention affects cross-architecture performance variance using models from the hybrid recurrent model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. Our analysis spans three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2), five attention ratios (0\\% pure recurrent, 4\\%, 7.7\\%, 14.3\\%, 25\\%), and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters). In the h$k$-1 notation used throughout, one attention layer is interleaved per every $k{+}1$ layers; h3-1 denotes 25\\% attention. All models within each scale share identical training data and hyperparameters; they differ only in architecture and attention configuration. We evaluate on 10 benchmarks spanning retrieval, comprehension, reasoning, and mixed task categories, plus one supplementary benchmark (COPA, $n = 100$).\n\n\\subsection{Task-Specific Convergence}\\label{ssec:task_convergence}\n\nWe first ask whether adding attention layers reduces performance differences between architectures, and whether any such reduction is uniform across tasks. For each benchmark, we compute the cross-architecture standard deviation ($\\sigma$) at each attention ratio and measure the Spearman rank correlation ($\\rho$) between attention ratio and $\\sigma$ across the five configurations.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:convergence} reports results for all benchmarks at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA is the only benchmark exhibiting monotonic convergence: $\\rho = -1.0$ (exact permutation test, $p = .017$, two-tailed, $n = 5$, 120 permutations). Cross-architecture $\\sigma$ drops from 0.024 to 0.005, a 79\\% reduction. The 95\\% BCa percentile bootstrap confidence interval for $\\rho$ is $[-1.0, -0.7]$ (10{,}000 resamples), which excludes zero. After Bonferroni correction for six pre-registered benchmarks, the adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict Bonferroni correction at $\\alpha = 0.05$. We therefore treat the confidence interval, rather than the corrected $p$-value, as the primary evidence for the LAMBADA convergence effect.\n\nThe task-specificity of this result is itself a finding. LAMBADA \\citep{paperno2016lambada} is a pure context retrieval task: predicting the final word of a passage requires encoding long-range dependencies but no multi-step reasoning. Attention provides direct token-level access to the full context, so convergence on a retrieval task is consistent with attention compensating for differences in how recurrent architectures store and recall contextual information. The remaining benchmarks span comprehension (BoolQ), reasoning (HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, PIQA), and mixed demands (ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, XStoryCloze). None shows significant convergence: all have $|\\rho| \\leq 0.7$ and $p \\geq .233$. Two mixed benchmarks (ARC-Easy, MMLU) exhibit moderate negative correlations ($\\rho = -0.7$) and high convergence ratios (7.70 and 2.27, respectively), but their non-monotonic trajectories prevent these correlations from reaching significance. The supplementary benchmark COPA shows a similar convergence ratio (5.57, $\\rho = -0.67$) but is limited by its small sample size. For tasks that involve reasoning or comprehension beyond retrieval, architectural differences in recurrent layers remain performance-relevant regardless of attention allocation (Figure~\\ref{fig:task_specificity}). We note that the decoupling analysis (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) requires a benchmark exhibiting aggregate convergence as a prerequisite. Among our ten benchmarks, only LAMBADA satisfies this condition, so our decoupling finding is necessarily demonstrated on LAMBADA alone. This reflects the task-specificity of aggregate convergence rather than a limitation of the decoupling phenomenon itself.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig2_task_specificity.pdf}\n\\caption{Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ for each benchmark at 1.3B scale. LAMBADA ($\\rho = -1.0$; BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$) is the only benchmark with a negative correlation whose confidence interval excludes zero. Benchmarks are grouped by task category.}\n\\label{fig:task_specificity}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{table*}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-architecture convergence across benchmarks (1.3B scale). $\\rho$: Spearman correlation between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ across five configurations. $p$: exact permutation test (two-tailed, 120 permutations). $\\sigma$ values reported $\\times 10^{2}$ (accuracy percentage points). Ratio: $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}} / \\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$. \\textbf{Bold}: only benchmark with $\\rho = -1.0$.}\n\\label{tab:convergence}\n\\begin{tabular}{llrrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & Category & $\\rho$ & $p$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{pure}}$ & $\\sigma_{\\text{h3-1}}$ & Ratio \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\textbf{LAMBADA} & \\textbf{Retrieval} & $\\mathbf{-1.0}$ & \\textbf{.017} & \\textbf{2.41} & \\textbf{0.49} & \\textbf{4.87} \\\\\nBoolQ & Comprehension & $0.2$ & .783 & 1.63 & 2.36 & 0.69 \\\\\nARC-C & Reasoning & $0.6$ & .350 & 0.96 & 1.32 & 0.72 \\\\\nHellaSwag & Reasoning & $0.3$ & .683 & 1.17 & 1.18 & 0.99 \\\\\nPIQA & Reasoning & $-0.5$ & .450 & 0.54 & 0.34 & 1.59 \\\\\nWinoGrande & Reasoning & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.67 & 0.78 & 0.86 \\\\\nARC-E & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 1.00 & 0.13 & 7.70 \\\\\nMMLU & Mixed & $-0.7$ & .233 & 0.92 & 0.41 & 2.27 \\\\\nSciQ & Mixed & $0.1$ & .950 & 0.87 & 0.74 & 1.19 \\\\\nXStoryCloze & Mixed & $-0.6$ & .350 & 0.82 & 0.55 & 1.49 \\\\\n\\midrule\nCOPA$^{\\dagger}$ & Reasoning & $-0.67$ & .267 & 2.63 & 0.47 & 5.57 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\multicolumn{7}{l}{\\footnotesize $^{\\dagger}$Supplementary ($n = 100$); excluded from multiple comparison correction.}\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table*}\n\n\\subsection{Scale-Dependent Convergence}\\label{ssec:scale_convergence}\n\nWe compare the LAMBADA convergence pattern at two scales. At 1.3B parameters, convergence is monotonic: $\\sigma$ decreases at every attention level from 0\\% through 25\\% ($\\rho = -1.0$). At 340M, convergence is partial. $\\sigma$ reaches a maximum reduction of 56\\% at intermediate attention ratios, but rebounds at h3-1, yielding a net endpoint reduction of 36\\%: the trajectory is non-monotonic, and the highest-attention model does not achieve the lowest cross-architecture variance. One possible interpretation is that at smaller scale, the capacity overhead of attention layers creates interference that offsets the retrieval benefit.\n\nThis contrast indicates that sufficient scale is a necessary condition for complete equalization on LAMBADA. We emphasize that this is a binary observation from two data points, not a scaling law. Characterizing the functional relationship between scale and convergence requires intermediate scales beyond the scope of this study. Despite the aggregate divergence, mean pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects is similar at both scales. At 340M, mean $J$ across the three architecture pairs is 0.094 for helped and 0.090 for hurt; at 1.3B, the corresponding values are 0.100 and 0.093. Per-example architecture specificity is stable across scales even when aggregate convergence trajectories differ, extending the decoupling observation from the attention-ratio dimension (\\S\\ref{ssec:decoupling}) to the scale dimension.\n\n\\subsection{Per-Example Divergence}\\label{ssec:per_example}\n\nThe aggregate convergence on LAMBADA raises a question: do architectures converge by improving on the same examples, or do they help and hurt different examples when attention is added? We compare each architecture's pure-recurrent baseline against its h3-1 hybrid variant on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples, classifying each example as helped (correct only in hybrid), hurt (correct only in pure), or unchanged.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:per_example} reports the results. Each architecture displays a distinct profile of per-example changes. GDN experiences a net loss of 153 examples (462 helped, 615 hurt), RetNet a net loss of 69 (426 helped, 495 hurt), and HGRN2 a net gain of 153 (548 helped, 395 hurt). The directional asymmetry is notable: HGRN2 is the only architecture for which attention produces a net benefit at the per-example level, while GDN and RetNet both lose more examples than they gain. Pairwise Jaccard similarity between any two architectures' affected sets is low: mean pairwise $\\bar{J}$ reaches at most $0.104$ for helped sets and $0.096$ for hurt sets, indicating that approximately 90\\% of each union set is non-shared. While overlap exceeds the independence baseline ($J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.05$), consistent with the structured cross-architecture patterns detected by the Kruskal--Wallis test below, the dominant pattern remains architecture-specific divergence. Permutation tests confirm that all pairwise Jaccard indices significantly exceed the independence baseline ($z > 5.9$, $p < .0001$; Appendix~\\ref{app:jaccard_permutation}).\n\nWe tested whether surface-level features (token count, word frequency, syntactic complexity) predict which examples are helped or hurt within a given architecture. No feature reliably separates helped from hurt examples for any single architecture. A cross-architecture analysis, however, reveals structure in the divergence. A Kruskal-Wallis test on the hurt-example distributions across architectures is significant ($p = .0005$), and the corresponding test for helped examples also reaches significance ($p = .020$). The architecture-specific patterns of per-example effects are therefore structured rather than random, even though the source of this structure lies beyond simple surface features. This structured disagreement at the individual-example level, despite similar aggregate performance, connects to the underspecification phenomenon documented by \\citet{damour2022underspecification}.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-example effects of attention (pure $\\to$ h3-1) on 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples at 1.3B scale. $\\bar{J}$: mean pairwise Jaccard similarity with other architectures across helped and hurt sets.}\n\\label{tab:per_example}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & Helped & Hurt & Net & $\\bar{J}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGDN & 462 & 615 & $-$153 & .096 \\\\\nRetNet & 426 & 495 & $-$69 & .100 \\\\\nHGRN2 & 548 & 395 & $+$153 & .104 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Decoupling}\\label{ssec:decoupling}\n\nThe central finding of this paper is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales as attention ratio increases (Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}).\n\nCross-architecture $\\sigma$ decreases gradually across the five attention ratios: $0.024 \\to 0.016 \\to 0.014 \\to 0.006 \\to 0.005$. Each additional attention layer provides incremental reduction. In contrast, pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' per-example effects saturates at the first attention level (4\\% attention): approximately 0.095 for helped examples, with no further increase at higher ratios. Net asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles increases from 0.013 at 4\\% attention to 0.040 at 25\\%.\n\nThese three quantities follow distinct trajectories. Aggregate variance requires progressively more attention to reduce. Per-example overlap reaches its ceiling with minimal attention. The asymmetry between architectures' per-example profiles grows as attention increases. This separation of timescales constitutes a decoupling: attention reduces the aggregate performance gap but does not homogenize per-example behavior. The per-example divergence in fact increases with attention ratio.\n\nBootstrap confidence intervals (10{,}000 resamples over examples) provide statistical confirmation of the decoupling. The 95\\% CI for cross-architecture $\\sigma$ does not overlap between the pure configuration $[0.019, 0.030]$ and h3-1 $[0.002, 0.011]$, confirming significant aggregate convergence. In contrast, the 95\\% CIs for mean pairwise Jaccard overlap completely across all four non-zero attention ratios (helped: $[0.08, 0.11]$ at each ratio; hurt: $[0.07, 0.11]$), statistically confirming the saturation pattern visible in Figure~\\ref{fig:decoupling}.\n\nAn analysis of attention weight patterns provides partial insight into this decoupling. At layer 2 of the 1.3B h3-1 models, GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine similarity 0.995), yet their per-example effects differ substantially. HGRN2 attention patterns diverge from the other architectures at early layers (cosine $\\sim$0.20) but converge at deeper layers ($>$0.94 at layer 20). Attention entropy does not differ between helped and hurt examples, which indicates that the per-example effects originate in the recurrent layers rather than in the attention mechanism itself. Full results appear in Appendix~D.\n\n\\subsection{Robustness}\\label{ssec:robustness}\n\nRemoving GDN and recomputing all correlations weakens the LAMBADA result from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$) and reverses MMLU from $\\rho = -0.7$ to $\\rho = +0.6$, confirming that individual architectures influence the aggregate trends. The weakening from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$ is confounded with the reduction from $n = 3$ to $n = 2$ architectures; with only two architectures, the minimum achievable $|\\rho|$ for a non-perfect monotonic relationship is $0.5$, limiting statistical resolution. Full sensitivity analyses, including bootstrap degenerate-resample treatment, appear in Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity}.\n",
        "numLines": 89,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 89
      }
    },

        "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\nWe present the first systematic per-example analysis of how attention affects cross-architecture agreement in hybrid recurrent models. The central result is that aggregate convergence and per-example divergence are decoupled: these two quantities follow different trajectories as attention increases.\n\nThree findings support this conclusion. Convergence is task-specific: only LAMBADA shows monotonic reduction in cross-architecture variance among ten benchmarks. Convergence depends on scale: complete at 1.3B but partial and non-monotonic at 340M. Aggregate convergence and per-example divergence operate on different timescales: cross-architecture variance decreases gradually with attention, but per-example overlap saturates at the lowest attention level and does not increase further.\n\nThese results carry a practical consequence: choosing between recurrent backbones on aggregate benchmark scores can obscure per-example behavioral differences that persist after aggregate metrics converge. This connects to underspecification \\citep{damour2022underspecification}: architectural variation produces per-prediction divergence analogous to what prior work attributed to random initialization.\n\nFuture work could establish causal links through attention head ablation, test generality across other hybrid families (Jamba, RWKV-6), and examine whether fine-tuning or additional scale points alter the decoupling pattern.\n",
        "numLines": 10,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 10
      }
    },

    "content": {

      "file": {
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/main.tex",
        "content": "\\documentclass[11pt]{article}\n\n\\usepackage[review]{acl}\n\n\\usepackage{times}\n\\usepackage{latexsym}\n\\usepackage[T1]{fontenc}\n\\usepackage[utf8]{inputenc}\n\\usepackage{microtype}\n\\usepackage{graphicx}\n\\usepackage{booktabs}\n\\usepackage{amsmath}\n\\usepackage{multirow}\n\\usepackage{xcolor}\n\n\\title{Aggregate Convergence, Per-Example Divergence: Decoupled Effects of Attention in Hybrid Recurrent Architectures}\n\n\\author{Anonymous}\n\n\\begin{document}\n\\maketitle\n\n\\begin{abstract}\nHybrid recurrent architectures that interleave attention layers with linear recurrence are increasingly standard, yet how attention affects per-example behavior across different recurrent backbones remains poorly quantified. We conduct a systematic analysis using the controlled model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}: three architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios and two scales (340M and 1.3B parameters), evaluated on 10 benchmarks with 5{,}153 per-example comparisons on LAMBADA. Of the 10 benchmarks, only LAMBADA exhibits cross-architecture convergence ($\\rho = -1.0$, BCa 95\\% CI $[-1.0, -0.7]$; cross-architecture standard deviation drops 79\\%). The Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ does not survive strict correction; the confidence interval serves as primary evidence. This convergence is complete at 1.3B but partial and non-monotonic at 340M; sufficient scale appears necessary for full equalization. Despite aggregate convergence, per-example helped and hurt sets are nearly disjoint across architectures (Jaccard similarity $\\leq 0.11$), and this overlap saturates at minimal (4\\%) attention. Aggregate performance equalizes progressively, while per-example specificity is fixed from the earliest attention allocation. These results quantify underspecification in hybrid architecture design: aggregate equivalence does not imply per-example behavioral interchangeability.\n\\end{abstract}\n\n\\input{sections/introduction}\n\n\\input{sections/related_work}\n\n\\input{sections/method}\n\n\\input{sections/experiments}\n\n\\input{sections/conclusion}\n\n\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur analysis has several limitations that constrain the scope of its conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Statistical power.} With only $n = 5$ attention ratios, the minimum achievable two-tailed $p$-value under an exact permutation test is approximately 0.008. This ceiling limits our ability to detect weak convergence effects and contributes to the Bonferroni-adjusted $p = .100$ for LAMBADA not reaching conventional significance. We report confidence intervals as primary evidence to partially address this constraint.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-example scope.} Our per-example decomposition is limited to LAMBADA, the only benchmark with statistically supported convergence. Extending helped/hurt analysis to benchmarks without convergence would not test the central claim, but examining per-example effects on tasks with moderate but non-significant convergence (e.g., ARC-Easy, MMLU) could reveal whether the decoupling pattern generalizes.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale coverage.} We compare two scale points (340M and 1.3B parameters). The contrast between complete convergence at 1.3B and partial convergence at 340M is a binary observation, not a scaling law. Intermediate and larger scales would be needed to characterize the functional relationship between model capacity and convergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Architecture coverage.} Our study examines three recurrent architectures (GatedDeltaNet, RetNet, HGRN2) from one model zoo. Other hybrid families, including Jamba, RWKV-6, and Hymba, use different mixing strategies that may produce different per-example patterns. Generalization beyond these three architectures requires additional controlled experiments.\n\n\\paragraph{Observational design.} Our attention weight analysis is descriptive, not causal. We report cosine similarities and entropy differences between helped and hurt examples but cannot determine whether attention patterns cause the per-example effects or merely correlate with them. Interventional methods such as attention head ablation would be necessary to establish causal links.\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-training only.} All models in our study are evaluated after pre-training without fine-tuning. Per-example effects may change under task-specific adaptation, and the decoupling pattern we observe may not persist after fine-tuning.\n\n\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work analyzes publicly available pretrained language models from the model zoo of \\citet{wang2025hybrid}. No new data collection, human subjects research, or model training is involved. We identify per-example behavioral differences across architectures, which may inform practitioners about the limitations of aggregate benchmark comparisons but does not introduce new risks.\n\n\\bibliography{references}\n\n\\appendix\n\\input{sections/appendix}\n\n\\end{document}\n",
        "numLines": 63,

    "content": {

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/appendix.tex",
        "content": "% Appendix sections for the paper.\n\n\\section{Pre-registration Details}\\label{app:benchmark_details}\n\nThe task taxonomy used in \\S\\ref{ssec:task_taxonomy} was defined prior to running any convergence analyses. Each benchmark was assigned to exactly one of four categories based on the primary cognitive demand of the task:\n\n\\begin{itemize}\n    \\item \\textbf{Retrieval}: tasks requiring prediction from long-range context without multi-step reasoning (LAMBADA).\n    \\item \\textbf{Comprehension}: reading comprehension with explicit passage support (BoolQ).\n    \\item \\textbf{Reasoning}: tasks requiring commonsense inference or multi-step reasoning (HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, PIQA).\n    \\item \\textbf{Mixed}: tasks combining retrieval, comprehension, and reasoning demands (ARC-Easy, MMLU, SciQ, XStoryCloze).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{Pre-registered benchmark set.} Six benchmarks were pre-registered for Bonferroni correction: LAMBADA, HellaSwag, ARC-Challenge, WinoGrande, ARC-Easy, and MMLU. These were selected as representative of retrieval, reasoning, and mixed categories before any convergence analysis was performed. The Bonferroni-adjusted significance threshold is $\\alpha / 6 = 0.0083$.\n\n\\paragraph{Exploratory benchmarks.} Four additional benchmarks (BoolQ, PIQA, SciQ, XStoryCloze) were added as exploratory tests. These are corrected separately across all 10 benchmarks (adjusted threshold $\\alpha / 10 = 0.005$) and reported as exploratory throughout the paper.\n\n\\paragraph{Supplementary benchmark.} COPA ($n = 100$) is treated as supplementary due to its small sample size. It is excluded from both correction families and reported separately with the $\\dagger$ annotation in Table~\\ref{tab:convergence}.\n\n\\section{Sensitivity Analyses}\\label{app:sensitivity}\n\n\\subsection{Bootstrap Confidence Intervals}\n\nWe construct 95\\% bootstrap confidence intervals for the Spearman $\\rho$ between attention ratio and cross-architecture $\\sigma$ on LAMBADA using 10{,}000 resamples at the architecture level ($n = 3$ architectures). Of these resamples, 11.25\\% are degenerate: they consist of same-architecture triplicates, which produce a constant $\\sigma$ vector and therefore an undefined $\\rho$ (division by zero in the rank correlation formula). These are excluded from the confidence interval computation because they carry no information about the correlation, not because they represent $\\rho = 0$.\n\nThe BCa (bias-corrected and accelerated) correction degenerates at the $\\rho = -1.0$ boundary because the acceleration parameter requires finite jackknife variance, which collapses when the point estimate is at the distributional limit. We therefore report percentile confidence intervals instead. From the 88.75\\% informative resamples, the 95\\% percentile CI is $[-1.0, -0.7]$, which excludes zero.\n\n\\subsection{GDN Exclusion Sensitivity}\n\nThe GDN h3-1 configuration uses 16 attention heads rather than 32 (see Appendix~\\ref{app:gdn_confound}). To assess the influence of this confound, we recompute all convergence correlations after excluding GDN, leaving two architectures (RetNet, HGRN2) at five attention ratios.\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:gdn_sensitivity} reports the results for benchmarks where the correlation changes by $|\\Delta\\rho| \\geq 0.3$. LAMBADA weakens from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$ ($p = .233$), consistent with GDN contributing to the convergence signal. MMLU reverses from $\\rho = -0.7$ to $\\rho = +0.6$, confirming that the moderate negative correlation on MMLU depends on GDN's inclusion and should not be interpreted as evidence of convergence.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Effect of GDN exclusion on Spearman $\\rho$ (attention ratio vs.\\ cross-architecture $\\sigma$) at 1.3B scale. Only benchmarks with $|\\Delta\\rho| \\geq 0.3$ shown.}\n\\label{tab:gdn_sensitivity}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrrr}\n\\toprule\nBenchmark & $\\rho_{\\text{all}}$ & $\\rho_{\\text{no-GDN}}$ & $\\Delta\\rho$ & $p_{\\text{no-GDN}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nLAMBADA & $-1.0$ & $-0.7$ & $+0.3$ & .233 \\\\\nMMLU & $-0.7$ & $+0.6$ & $+1.3$ & .350 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{GDN Attention Head Confound}\\label{app:gdn_confound}\n\nIn the \\citet{wang2025hybrid} model zoo, the GDN h3-1 configuration at 1.3B scale uses 16 attention heads per attention layer, while all other hybrid configurations (across GDN, RetNet, and HGRN2) use 32 attention heads. This is a design choice in the original model zoo, not an error. The reduced head count changes the per-head dimension and may affect the attention capacity of GDN h3-1 relative to other configurations.\n\nThis confound is relevant because the convergence analysis measures cross-architecture $\\sigma$ at each attention ratio. At the h3-1 ratio (25\\% attention), GDN's attention layers have a different internal structure than those of RetNet and HGRN2, which could contribute to variance reduction (or inflation) for reasons unrelated to the recurrent--attention interaction under study.\n\nThe sensitivity analysis in Appendix~\\ref{app:sensitivity} shows that removing GDN weakens the LAMBADA convergence correlation from $\\rho = -1.0$ to $\\rho = -0.7$. This weakening is partially attributable to the reduced sample size ($n = 2$ architectures) and partially to the confound itself. We cannot separate these two effects with the available data.\n\n\\section{Attention Weight Analysis}\\label{app:attention_patterns}\n\nWe extract attention weight matrices from the 1.3B h3-1 models of all three architectures using forward hooks on each attention layer. For each layer, we compute the mean attention distribution across all 5{,}153 LAMBADA examples and measure cross-architecture similarity via cosine similarity.\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Architecture Cosine Similarity}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:attn_cosine} reports cosine similarity between mean attention distributions at selected layers and averaged across all layers.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cosine similarity between mean attention weight distributions of architecture pairs at selected layers (1.3B h3-1 models on LAMBADA).}\n\\label{tab:attn_cosine}\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lrrr}\n\\toprule\nLayer & GDN--RetNet & GDN--HGRN2 & HGRN2--RetNet \\\\\n\\midrule\nL2 (early) & 0.995 & 0.244 & 0.196 \\\\\nL20 (deep) & 0.986 & 0.942 & 0.943 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean (all layers) & 0.905 & 0.766 & 0.698 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}%\n}\n\\end{table}\n\nAt layer 2, GDN and RetNet produce nearly identical attention distributions (cosine 0.995), while HGRN2 diverges from both (cosine $< 0.25$). By layer 20, all three architectures converge to similar attention patterns (cosine $> 0.94$). The layer-wise trajectory suggests that early-layer attention patterns are shaped by the preceding recurrent layers (which differ across architectures), while deeper layers converge regardless of the recurrent substrate.\n\n\\subsection{Helped vs.\\ Hurt Attention Entropy}\n\nTo test whether the attention mechanism itself differs between helped and hurt examples, we compute the Shannon entropy of the attention distribution for each example at each attention layer and compare the mean entropy of helped examples against hurt examples within each architecture.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Mean attention entropy difference (helped $-$ hurt) averaged across attention layers (1.3B h3-1 models on LAMBADA). Values near zero indicate that the attention mechanism does not discriminate between helped and hurt examples.}\n\\label{tab:attn_entropy}\n\\begin{tabular}{lr}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & $\\Delta H$ (helped $-$ hurt) \\\\\n\\midrule\nGDN & $-0.0009$ \\\\\nRetNet & $+0.0179$ \\\\\nHGRN2 & $+0.0020$ \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll entropy differences are negligible (Table~\\ref{tab:attn_entropy}). The attention mechanism allocates similar entropy to helped and hurt examples, consistent with the interpretation that per-example effects originate in the recurrent layers rather than in the attention layers.\n\n\\subsection{Last-5 Attention Mass}\n\nWe also measure the fraction of attention mass allocated to the last five tokens, which captures whether the attention mechanism concentrates differently on recent context for helped vs.\\ hurt examples.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Mean attention mass on last-5 tokens for helped and hurt examples, averaged across attention layers (1.3B h3-1 models on LAMBADA).}\n\\label{tab:attn_last5}\n\\begin{tabular}{lrr}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & Helped & Hurt \\\\\n\\midrule\nGDN & 0.0834 & 0.0831 \\\\\nRetNet & 0.1578 & 0.1591 \\\\\nHGRN2 & 0.0453 & 0.0467 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nNo architecture shows a meaningful difference in last-5 attention mass between helped and hurt examples (Table~\\ref{tab:attn_last5}). This further supports the conclusion that the per-example divergence is not driven by differential attention allocation.\n\n\\section{Cross-Scale Per-Example Comparisons}\\label{app:cross_scale}\n\n\\subsection{Within-Scale Pairwise Jaccard}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:within_scale_jaccard} reports the within-scale pairwise Jaccard similarity between architectures' helped and hurt sets at both scales. Mean values are similar across scales (340M: 0.094/0.090; 1.3B: 0.100/0.093), confirming that per-example architecture specificity is approximately scale-invariant.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\small\n\\caption{Within-scale pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example effects (pure $\\to$ h3-1) on LAMBADA. Each row compares the helped (or hurt) sets of two architectures at the same scale.}\n\\label{tab:within_scale_jaccard}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nScale & Pair & $J_{\\text{helped}}$ & $J_{\\text{hurt}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{340M} & GDN--RetNet & 0.092 & 0.109 \\\\\n& GDN--HGRN2 & 0.084 & 0.075 \\\\\n& RetNet--HGRN2 & 0.108 & 0.085 \\\\\n& \\textit{Mean} & \\textit{0.094} & \\textit{0.090} \\\\\n\\midrule\n\\multirow{4}{*}{1.3B} & GDN--RetNet & 0.092 & 0.103 \\\\\n& GDN--HGRN2 & 0.100 & 0.089 \\\\\n& RetNet--HGRN2 & 0.108 & 0.087 \\\\\n& \\textit{Mean} & \\textit{0.100} & \\textit{0.093} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\subsection{Cross-Scale Pairwise Jaccard}\n\nFor each architecture, we compare the helped and hurt sets of the pure $\\to$ h3-1 transition at 340M and 1.3B scale on LAMBADA.\nThe comparison uses the same classification scheme as \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}: an example is ``helped'' if it is correct only in the hybrid variant and ``hurt'' if correct only in the pure baseline.\nAll pairwise Jaccard values fall below 0.10, indicating that scale produces per-example underspecification analogous to the cross-architecture effect reported in \\S\\ref{ssec:per_example}.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Cross-scale pairwise Jaccard similarity of per-example effects (pure $\\to$ h3-1) between 340M and 1.3B on LAMBADA. All values below 0.10.}\n\\label{tab:cross_scale_jaccard}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcc}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & Scale Pair & $J_{\\text{helped}}$ & $J_{\\text{hurt}}$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGDN & 340M $\\leftrightarrow$ 1.3B & 0.047 & 0.087 \\\\\nRetNet & 340M $\\leftrightarrow$ 1.3B & 0.059 & 0.073 \\\\\nHGRN2 & 340M $\\leftrightarrow$ 1.3B & 0.089 & 0.055 \\\\\n\\midrule\nMean & & 0.065 & 0.072 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Jaccard Permutation Test}\\label{app:jaccard_permutation}\n\nTo assess whether the observed pairwise Jaccard similarities exceed what would be expected under independence, we perform a permutation test. For each architecture pair at 1.3B h3-1, we hold the helped (or hurt) set sizes fixed and randomly assign example indices to each set (10{,}000 permutations, seed 42). Table~\\ref{tab:jaccard_perm} reports the observed $J$, the null baseline $J_{\\text{null}}$ (mean over permutations), and the $z$-score.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Permutation test for pairwise Jaccard similarity (1.3B h3-1 on LAMBADA, 10{,}000 permutations). All observed values significantly exceed the independence baseline.}\n\\label{tab:jaccard_perm}\n\\begin{tabular}{llcccc}\n\\toprule\nCategory & Pair & $J_{\\text{obs}}$ & $J_{\\text{null}}$ & $z$ & $p$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nHelped & GDN vs HGRN2 & .100 & .051 & 7.11 & $<$.0001 \\\\\n & GDN vs RetNet & .092 & .045 & 6.75 & $<$.0001 \\\\\n & HGRN2 vs RetNet & .108 & .049 & 8.61 & $<$.0001 \\\\\n\\midrule\nHurt & GDN vs HGRN2 & .088 & .049 & 5.91 & $<$.0001 \\\\\n & GDN vs RetNet & .103 & .056 & 6.85 & $<$.0001 \\\\\n & HGRN2 vs RetNet & .087 & .045 & 6.05 & $<$.0001 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll six comparisons yield $z > 5.9$ ($p < .0001$). The null baseline averages $J_{\\text{null}} \\approx 0.049$; observed values are approximately twice this level. The overlap between architectures' per-example effect sets is therefore structured rather than a product of random set overlap given the set sizes.\n\n\\section{Transformer Entrainment Comparison}\\label{app:transformer_entrainment}\n\nTo test whether attention layers make hybrid models' per-example behavior converge toward a pure Transformer, we compare the correct-example sets of each architecture's pure and h3-1 variants against a 1.3B Transformer baseline (accuracy 48.1\\%) on LAMBADA. Table~\\ref{tab:transformer_jaccard} reports the Jaccard similarity between each model's correct set and the Transformer's correct set.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Jaccard similarity between each model's correct set and the Transformer correct set on LAMBADA (1.3B scale). All three h3-1 hybrids are more similar to the Transformer than their pure counterparts.}\n\\label{tab:transformer_jaccard}\n\\begin{tabular}{lccr}\n\\toprule\nArchitecture & $J$(pure, Trans) & $J$(h3-1, Trans) & $\\Delta$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nGDN & 0.645 & 0.649 & $+$0.005 \\\\\nRetNet & 0.679 & 0.694 & $+$0.015 \\\\\nHGRN2 & 0.672 & 0.701 & $+$0.029 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\nAll three architectures show positive $\\Delta$: the h3-1 correct set is more similar to the Transformer correct set than the pure correct set is. The effect is strongest for HGRN2 ($+$0.029) and weakest for GDN ($+$0.005). This ordering is consistent with HGRN2 being the only architecture with a positive net per-example effect from attention (Table~\\ref{tab:per_example}). The Transformer itself achieves the lowest accuracy among all models, so the convergence is not explained by hybrid models degrading toward Transformer-level performance; rather, adding attention shifts which specific examples the hybrid gets correct toward the Transformer's pattern.\n",
        "numLines": 223,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 223
      }
    },

  },

        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_2/projects/entrainment_gated_ft/docs/paper/sections/related_work.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Hybrid Recurrent Architectures.}\nThe design space for combining attention with recurrence has expanded along two axes: where mixing occurs and how much attention is allocated. Jamba \\citep{lieber2024jamba} interleaves Mamba and Transformer blocks across layers, while Griffin \\citep{de2024griffin} and RecurrentGemma \\citep{botev2024recurrentgemma} pair gated linear recurrences with local attention. RWKV-6 \\citep{peng2024eagle} adds matrix-valued recurrent states alongside attention-like token mixing, and Hymba \\citep{dong2024hymba} mixes attention and recurrence within individual heads rather than across layers. Mamba-2 \\citep{dao2024mamba2} establishes a formal duality between structured state spaces and attention. Across these designs, comparative evaluation has relied on aggregate benchmark scores. \\citet{wang2025hybrid} provide a controlled model zoo that holds training conditions constant while varying architecture and attention ratio, which enables direct comparison. We use this zoo to study behavioral effects of attention at both aggregate and per-example granularity.\n\n\\paragraph{Linear Recurrent Models.}\nLinear recurrent models evolved from structured state spaces to selective and gated variants. S4 \\citep{gu2022s4} introduced parameterized state-space layers with linear complexity for long-range dependencies, and \\citet{katharopoulos2020transformers} established a theoretical bridge by showing that softmax attention can be approximated by linear dot-product kernels. Mamba \\citep{gu2024mamba} extended S4 with input-dependent selection, which allows the recurrence to filter information based on content rather than position. Subsequent designs pursued different gating strategies: RetNet \\citep{sun2023retnet} uses multi-scale exponential decay, HGRN2 \\citep{qin2024hgrn2} applies gated linear recurrence with state expansion, and GatedDeltaNet \\citep{yang2024gateddeltanet} combines gated updates with delta rules. These three architectures serve as the recurrent backbones in our study, and their distinct parameterizations make them a natural test bed for whether attention produces uniform or architecture-specific behavioral effects.\n\n\\paragraph{Underspecification and Model Agreement.}\n\\citet{damour2022underspecification} formalized underspecification: a training pipeline admits multiple models with equivalent aggregate performance but different per-prediction behavior. Their study documented this across computer vision, medical imaging, and NLP, showing that standard model selection does not resolve the ambiguity. \\citet{mccoy2020berts} demonstrated a concrete NLP instance: BERT models trained from identical configurations but different random seeds achieve similar aggregate scores while diverging on which constructions each seed handles correctly. \\citet{fort2019deep} found that different initializations converge to distinct loss landscape modes with different generalization profiles. \\citet{kornblith2019similarity} proposed Centered Kernel Alignment (CKA) to measure representational similarity and showed that networks with different initializations learn partially different internal representations. These studies attribute per-prediction divergence to variation in initialization or training noise within a single architecture. Our work tests whether the same phenomenon arises when the source of variation is the recurrent backbone itself.\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble Diversity.}\nThe relationship between prediction diversity and ensemble performance has a long theoretical history. \\citet{krogh1994ensembles} proved that ensemble error decomposes into average individual error minus a diversity term, so disagreement among members directly reduces ensemble error. \\citet{kuncheva2003measures} surveyed ten pairwise diversity measures and found that no single measure reliably predicts ensemble accuracy, which limits the practical use of diversity for ensemble selection. \\citet{lakshminarayanan2017simple} showed that deep ensembles trained from different random initializations produce well-calibrated uncertainty estimates, and that diversity arises naturally from stochastic optimization without explicit diversity objectives. The hybrid architectures we study are not ensembles, but the per-example disagreement we observe between architectures parallels the diversity that ensemble theory predicts should reduce combined error.\n\n\\paragraph{Per-Example Analysis.}\nA growing line of work treats evaluation examples as non-exchangeable, assigning per-example difficulty or informativeness scores. \\citet{swayamdipta2020dataset} introduced dataset cartography, which maps each training example into a confidence-variability space and identifies easy, ambiguous, and hard regions of a dataset. \\citet{rodriguez2021evaluation} applied Item Response Theory from psychometrics to NLP benchmarks, estimating per-item difficulty and discriminability that reveal how informative each example is about model quality. \\citet{ethayarajh2022understanding} proposed V-usable information as a measure of dataset difficulty relative to a specific model family, formalizing the intuition that difficulty depends on the learner. These methods characterize examples through the lens of a single model or model family. Our helped/hurt decomposition is a cross-architecture variant: we classify each example by how adding attention changes each architecture's prediction, then measure whether architectures agree on which examples are affected.\n\n\\paragraph{Attention Interpretability.}\nWhether attention weights carry interpretive signal has been debated. \\citet{jain2019attention} argued that attention distributions do not reliably indicate input importance, since alternative weight configurations can produce identical outputs. \\citet{wiegreffe2019attention} countered that alternative explanations do not invalidate attention-based ones under appropriate conditions. \\citet{clark2019what} showed that specific BERT attention heads capture syntactic relations such as coreference, even if the full attention matrix is not a faithful global explanation. \\citet{olsson2022induction} identified induction heads, attention heads that implement in-context copying by attending to prior occurrences of the current token's context. Our attention weight analysis is observational: we report differences in attention entropy and cosine similarity between helped and hurt examples but do not claim causal status for attention patterns.\n",
        "numLines": 20,
        "startLine": 1,
        "totalLines": 20
      }
    },
